Test pyramid for a compiler+runtime: many cheap unit rewrites, fewer integration end-to-ends, and a differential/property/fuzz crown that pins the load-bearing invariant — the schedule and the passes change performance, never numerics. Every class below cites a Section-7 invariant (I-n).

Test taxonomy & budget

Class Scope Count target Runtime budget Gate
Unit per op / pass / primitive / type ~600 < 30 s every PR
Integration model→compile→run→oracle ~40 < 90 s every PR
Differential compiled vs oracle; executor vs VM; pre/post pass ~120 < 120 s every PR
Property (proptest) numerics/shape/aliasing/bounds ~25 props × 256 cases < 180 s every PR
Fuzz (cargo-fuzz / arbitrary) graph + legal-schedule gen 5 targets nightly ≥ 30 min/target nightly + corpus on PR
Conformance op + model corpus ledger corpus-sized < 5 min every PR
Bench (criterion) GFLOP/s, compile time ~30 nightly regression gate

1. Unit tests

Subject Crate What is asserted Invariant
Op type/shape inference tc-graph each GraphOp → exact TensorType (shape+dtype); broadcast, MatMul contraction, Conv2d formula, Reduce axis drop I-shape-sound
Pass before/after tc-pass golden IR snapshot pre/post const-fold, CSE, DCE, simplify, layout; node-count delta exact I-pass-preserves
Schedule primitive rewrite tc-schedule loop-nest structure after split/reorder/fuse/tile/vectorize/unroll/parallel/compute_at/inline matches expected Stmt tree; AxisId remap correct I-sched-legal
Codegen per op tc-codegen emitted C / SIMD Kernel.source compiles via cc; KernelSig (ptr+shape+stride args) stable; one golden emitted-kernel snapshot per op I-codegen-faithful
NDArray strided views tc-runtime reshape/transpose/slice are zero-copy (shared Storage, new strides, offset); element addressing offset+Σ idx_i·stride_i correct I-view-bounds
MemoryPlan liveness tc-runtime liveness intervals over topo order; two tensors share Storage ⟺ disjoint live ranges; total bytes ≤ naive sum I-no-alias-live
Allocator tc-runtime alloc/free balance; alignment (64B SIMD); no double-free I-no-alias-live
VM decode/dispatch tc-vm each VmInstruction updates register file as specced; If/Goto/Call/Ret control flow I-vm-sound

Golden snapshots: insta-style; tcc dump {graph,tir,scheduled,c,module} output is the snapshot surface — review on diff.

2. Integration tests

Full pipeline per fixture model: import → passes → fuse → lower → schedule → codegen → Module → GraphExecutor.run → compare oracle.

Fixture Exercises
add_chain elementwise fusion, MemoryPlan reuse
mlp (matmul+bias+relu) MatMul tile/vectorize, fuse epilogue
conv_relu_pool Conv2d layout transform, reduce fusion
dynamic_shape_loop VM path, runtime alloc, Dim::Dynamic resolution
onnx_subset_resnet_block importer + multi-pass + multi-kernel Module

Assert: numerics within tolerance (§ below), kernel count == expected after fusion, Module loads/runs both default and --features simd.

3. Differential testing

Three independent oracles must agree:

compiled Module ── vs ──> reference oracle (NumPy-style, pure-Rust)   [I-oracle]
GraphExecutor   ── vs ──> tc-vm Interpreter (same Kernels, same inputs) [I-exec≡vm]
IR pre-pass     ── vs ──> IR post-pass (interpret both, equal numerics) [I-pass-preserves]
  • Oracle: each GraphOp has a naive pure-Rust reference; whole-graph reference = composed op refs. Authoritative for correctness, never tuned.
  • Executor vs VM: same Module, same inputs, same outputs bit-comparable up to FP-reassoc tolerance.
  • Per-pass numeric diff: interpret Graph IR before and after each pass on random inputs → equal within tolerance (catches a miscompiling simplify/layout rule immediately).
  • Schedule-equivalence diff: baseline (naive) Kernel vs scheduled Kernel for the same PrimFunc → equal (I-sched-legal: schedule never changes result).

4. Tolerance contract

dtype rtol atol notes
F32 1e-5 1e-6 FP reassoc from vectorize/parallel reductions allowed within bound
F64 1e-12 1e-13
I32/I64/I8 exact exact bit-equal; any diff = bug
Bool exact exact

Reduction order changes (parallel/vectorize) are the only sanctioned source of F32 drift; if a diff exceeds rtol it is a miscompile, not noise.

5. Property testing (proptest)

Property Generator Assertion Invariant
Pass preserves numerics random typed Graph interpret(G) ≈ interpret(pass(G)) I-pass-preserves
Legal schedule preserves numerics PrimFunc + random legal primitive sequence run(naive) ≈ run(scheduled) I-sched-legal
Shape inference soundness random Graph inferred shapes match runtime tensor shapes; no None ty post-infer I-shape-sound
MemoryPlan never aliases live random Graph→plan ∀ overlapping live ranges → distinct Storage I-no-alias-live
NDArray views in bounds random shape/stride/slice every computed byte offset ∈ [0, Storage.bytes) I-view-bounds
split·fuse round-trip axis + factor fuse(split(ax,f)) ≡ original loop extent I-sched-legal
const-fold idempotent random Graph fold(fold(G)) == fold(G) I-pass-preserves

Shrinking: proptest minimizes failing Graph/schedule to smallest reproducer; persist as a regression fixture.

6. Fuzzing (cargo-fuzz)

Target Input Oracle checks
fuzz_graph arbitrary well-typed Graph no panic/UB through full pipeline; output ≈ oracle; no kernel OOB (ASan)
fuzz_schedule PrimFunc + legal-by-construction schedule no panic; scheduled ≈ naive; no OOB load/store
fuzz_importer bytes → ONNX-subset / textual graph no panic; reject malformed cleanly (no UB)
fuzz_codegen random scheduled TIR emitted-C compiles -Wall -Werror; scalar vs SIMD numeric parity
fuzz_vm random VmFunc bytecode no panic; bounded loops; no OOB register/heap access
  • Legal-schedule generator only emits primitive sequences that respect dependence (no reorder across a reduce init, valid tile factors) — fuzzing legal space tests miscompiles, not rejection.
  • Run under ASan/UBSan; OOB in an emitted C kernel is caught at the cc-compiled-with-sanitizer boundary.
  • Deterministic replay: every fuzz failure → seed + serialized input checked into tests/fuzz_corpus/; replayable as a normal #[test]. Corpus minimized and run on every PR.

7. Conformance ledger

conformance/ buckets: op corpus (every registered GraphOp × dtype × representative shapes) and model corpus (the fixture models + ONNX-subset graphs). Ledger conformance/ledger.toml:

[op.matmul.f32]   status = "pass"
[op.conv2d.f32]   status = "pass"
[model.resnet_blk] status = "known-fail"  reason = "layout-cost heuristic suboptimal, numerics OK"  issue = 142

States: pass / fail (blocks merge) / known-fail (tracked, must cite issue, must be numerically explainable not a silent miscompile). tcc check runs the ledger vs oracle and reports deltas; CI fails on any pass→fail regression or undocumented new fail.

8. Performance gates (criterion)

Metric Target Gate
MatMul (1024³ f32) GFLOP/s ≥ 30% of OpenBLAS sgemm bench regression < 5% PR-over-PR
Fused elementwise vs unfused ≥ 2× speedup assert fusion win
Vectorized vs scalar kernel ≥ 3× (avx2) assert SIMD path
tcc compile time (resnet block) < 2 s hard ceiling
Compiled vs naive baseline ≥ 5× geomean over corpus nightly trend

criterion baselines committed; >5% GFLOP/s regression or compile-time ceiling breach fails the nightly gate and pages the owner.

9. Coverage & CI

  • Coverage target: ≥ 85% line, ≥ 75% branch (cargo-llvm-cov) across all nine tc-* crates; tc-pass/tc-schedule/tc-codegen (highest miscompile risk) held to ≥ 90%.
  • CI matrix: {default, --features simd} × {debug, release}; sanitizer job (ASan/UBSan) on fuzz corpus; MSRV 1.78 build check.
  • Pre-merge gate = unit + integration + differential + property + conformance + corpus replay; bench + extended fuzz run nightly.

Build & Release Engineering

Reproducibility contract

Pin Mechanism Value / policy
Rust toolchain rust-toolchain.toml (committed) channel = "1.78.0" (MSRV == release channel), components = ["clippy","rustfmt"], profile = "minimal"
Kernel cc tc-codegen records compiler id/version in KernelSig provenance; CI pins clang-17 (Linux), Apple clang from a pinned Xcode CLT, MSVC excluded (CPU C path only) CC env locked per matrix leg; -O3 -march=<target.arch> -ffp-contract=off
FP determinism -ffp-contract=off + no -ffast-math preserves oracle tolerance (rtol 1e-5 / atol 1e-6); FMA contraction would drift f32 results
Deps Cargo.lock committed for tc-cli (binary); not committed for library crates reproducible tcc builds; libs resolve fresh
Source date SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH from the release git commit strips build timestamps from Module headers

Module artifacts are byte-reproducible only modulo the pinned cc: emitted C is deterministic (stable kernel naming, sorted buffer order); the object/.so step inherits cc determinism. CI asserts tcc compile model.txt twice → identical emitted C (diff of tcc dump c).

Build / test matrix

            stable(1.78)  beta   nightly   notes
x86_64-linux    ✓          ✓       fuzz     primary; AVX2 SimdBackend tested
aarch64-linux   ✓          —       —        NEON SimdBackend path
aarch64-macos   ✓          —       —        Apple clang, release artifact
x86_64-windows  build      —       —        codegen disabled (no pinned cc); compiler-only crates
Job Crates / scope Command Gate
build workspace cargo build --workspace --all-targets required
test workspace cargo test --workspace required
no_std tc-graph, tc-ir cores cargo check -p tc-graph -p tc-ir --no-default-features --target thumbv7em-none-eabi required (proves no_std + alloc purity)
fmt workspace cargo fmt --all --check required
clippy workspace cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings required
msrv workspace pinned 1.78.0 is the only stable leg → MSRV verified by construction required
fuzz graph + schedule generators cargo +nightly fuzz run <target> -- -max_total_time=300 nightly only (non-blocking on PR, blocking on merge queue)
bench criterion + GFLOP/s cargo bench -p tc-codegen -p tc-runtime informational; regression alert >5%
conformance numerical vs oracle tcc check --corpus oracle/ gated (needs cc + oracle data)
deny workspace cargo deny check required

conformance and bench require the pinned cc toolchain (kernels are compiled, not interpreted) and the reference-oracle dataset (op + model corpus, NumPy-generated golden .npy). The corpus is a separately versioned LFS/release asset (oracle-vN.tar.zst); the job is gated behind label run-conformance on PRs and runs unconditionally on main / tags. Missing corpus → job is skipped, never silently green.

Feature flags affecting CI legs

Feature Crate Effect CI
simd (default) tc-codegen portable-SIMD SimdBackend on in all legs
llvm (off) tc-codegen optional LLVM codegen path instead of C-source CBackend; pulls inkwell/llvm-sys one dedicated leg --features llvm (Linux, pinned LLVM 17); not in default matrix; release artifacts ship C path only
threads (default) tc-runtime thread-pool backing parallel axes / async dispatch on
oracle tc-cli links reference-oracle harness for check conformance leg only

LLVM is strictly optional and feature-gated: default builds, MSRV check, and shipped binaries never link LLVM. The llvm leg validates the alternate CodeGen::emit path produces oracle-equal results.

Supply chain: audit + SBOM

  • cargo deny check: advisories (RUSTSEC), licenses (allow MIT/Apache-2.0/BSD-3-Clause/Unicode-3.0), duplicate-version ban, source allowlist (crates.io only). deny.toml committed.
  • cargo audit on lockfile in a daily scheduled run (independent of PR matrix).
  • SBOM: tcc built with cargo auditable build --release → embeds dependency tree in the binary; cargo audit bin tcc re-derives it post-release. CycloneDX sbom.json exported per release via cargo cyclonedx and attached as a release asset.

Publication order + semver

Crates publish bottom-up (a crate publishes only after its deps are live on crates.io):

tc-graph → tc-ir → { tc-pass, tc-fuse } → tc-schedule → tc-codegen
        → tc-runtime → tc-vm → tc-cli
  • All nine crates share one workspace version, stamped from the git tag (v0.X.Y) via cargo set-version (release-plz / cargo-release). 0.x → minor = breaking per Cargo semver.
  • Public-API breaks tracked with cargo public-api diff in CI; a break without a minor bump fails the release job.
  • tc-graph/tc-ir/tc-vm IR enums are non-exhaustive (#[non_exhaustive] on GraphOp, Stmt, Expr, VmInstruction) so adding ops/instructions is non-breaking.

Binary release artifacts

Target Triple cc Notes
Linux x86_64 x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu clang-17 AVX2 SimdBackend
Linux aarch64 aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu clang-17 (cross) NEON
macOS aarch64 aarch64-apple-darwin Apple clang NEON, codesigned + notarized
  • tcc shipped as tcc-<ver>-<triple>.tar.zst + .sha256 + .sig (cosign keyless / Sigstore; minisign fallback). cosign verify-blob instructions in release notes.
  • The shipped tcc requires a host cc at runtime for compile/tune (it emits C and shells out); release notes state the supported cc versions and the --emit-c-only escape hatch for hosts without a compiler.

Module artifact versioning

  • Module serialized format carries MODULE_ABI = u32 header + producing tcc semver + Target { arch, features } + cc provenance.
  • tc-runtime GraphExecutor::load / tc-vm Interpreter::load reject a Module whose MODULE_ABI ≠ runtime ABI, or whose Target.features aren't a subset of the host's detected features (avoids running an AVX2 kernel on a non-AVX2 host).
  • ABI bumps on any change to KernelSig layout (pointer/shape/stride ordering) or MemoryPlan encoding.

Workflow topology — .github/workflows/

File Trigger Jobs
ci.yml push, PR build, test, no_std, fmt, clippy, deny — across the build matrix
conformance.yml push, PR (label run-conformance) + main/tags unconditionally tcc check --corpus conformance/ vs the pinned oracle; gated on PRs (needs cc + oracle data), unconditional on main/tags
fuzz.yml nightly cron + merge-queue cargo fuzz over the five fuzz_targets/*.rs (300s/target), corpus cached + minimized; failure uploads crashing input
bench.yml PR (label run-bench) + nightly criterion + GFLOP/s vs naive baseline; posts % peak / % BLAS table; alerts on >5% regression
release.yml tag v* build+sign per-target binaries, build Module test artifacts, cargo auditable + CycloneDX SBOM, publish crates in the locked order, create GitHub release with assets + cosign sigs

release.yml publish step is fail-stop and ordered: a crates.io failure at any node aborts before publishing dependents, preventing a half-published workspace.

Performance Engineering

Performance is a first-class correctness property: a schedule that is fast but wrong is rejected by the oracle (§13). All numbers below tie to the §4 targets. Default measurement host: x86_64, AVX2, 8 physical cores, 32 KB L1d / 256 KB L2 / 16 MB L3, single-socket; reported per-kernel and end-to-end.

Performance targets (recap from §4)

Each row carries an M# ID so other sections (Hard Questions, ADRs, runbooks) can cite it as a row token rather than a dead §-number.

ID Workload Metric Naive baseline Target (this compiler) Stretch
M1 MatMul 1024³ f32 GFLOP/s, % BLAS ~2 GFLOP/s ≥35% OpenBLAS ≥55%
M2 Conv2d 1×64×56×56, 3×3 GFLOP/s ~1.5 ≥25% BLAS-via-im2col ≥40%
M3 elementwise Add/Relu 16M f32 GB/s, % STREAM ~scalar ≥80% mem BW ≥90%
M4 Reduce (sum, 16M→1) GB/s ~scalar ≥70% mem BW ≥85%
M5 fused Conv+Bias+Relu vs unfused 3 kernels ≥1.6× via fusion ≥2.0×
M6 compile (ResNet-subset) wall ms — ≤800 ms ≤400 ms
M7 executor steady-state alloc bytes/iter — 0 0
M8 f32 numerical rtol/atol — 1e-5 / 1e-6 —
M9 SIMD utilization (vectorized elementwise) 256b_packed_single ÷ total FP ops — ≥0.85 ≥0.92
M10 SIMD utilization (MatMul microkernel) 256b_packed_single ÷ total FP ops — ≥0.70 ≥0.80
M11 tiled-matmul cache-miss rate L1d miss rate untiled ~L2-bound ≤2% ≤1%
M12 MemoryPlan reuse ratio sum(tensor_bytes) / peak_resident_bytes 1× (no reuse) ≥2× on model corpus ≥3×

Benchmarking strategy

  • Harness: criterion micro-benches under benches/, one group per kernel class: matmul, conv2d, elementwise, reduce. Each sweeps sizes; reports time → derived GFLOP/s and % of baseline.
  • Baselines (two, always reported): (1) naive-blocked — the default unscheduled nested-loop PrimFunc from Phase 6 lowering (correctness floor, perf floor); (2) BLAS reference — OpenBLAS sgemm / im2col+sgemm for matmul/conv only (behind bench-blas feature; absent → row shows n/a).
  • Size sweeps (FLOPs annotated for GFLOP/s):
Kernel Sizes swept FLOP formula
MatMul M,N,K ∈ {128, 256, 512, 1024, 2048} 2·M·N·K
Conv2d C∈{16,64,256}, HW∈{14,28,56}, k=3 2·N·Cout·Cin·k²·Hout·Wout
elementwise n ∈ {1K, 64K, 1M, 16M} n (bytes = 3·n·sizeof)
Reduce n ∈ {1K, 64K, 1M, 16M} n (bytes = n·sizeof)
  • Compile-time benches: per-pass wall time over the §13 model corpus, reported as a stacked table (shape-infer / const-fold / CSE / DCE / simplify / layout / fusion / lower / schedule / codegen / cc-invoke). cc subprocess time tracked separately (dominant, often 60–80% of compile). Regression gate: ±10% per pass vs committed baseline JSON in CI.
  • End-to-end: tcc bench <model> reports compile ms + per-iteration run ms + GFLOP/s, warm (≥30 iters after 5 warmup), median + p99.

Profiling methodology

Tool Target Signal extracted
perf stat hot kernel cycles, IPC, L1-dcache-load-misses, LLC-load-misses, fp_arith_inst_retired.256b_packed_single
perf record + flamegraph run + compile pipeline hot frames; confirm cc / pass split
cargo flamegraph tcc compile/run Rust-side hotspots (pass infra, dispatch)
valgrind --tool=cachegrind small repro exact miss counts when HW counters noisy
  • SIMD utilization = 256b_packed_single retired ÷ total FP ops; target ≥0.85 for vectorized elementwise, ≥0.70 for matmul microkernel. Low value → vectorize axis not innermost / not a multiple of lane width → emit remainder-loop warning.
  • Cache-miss budget: tiled matmul L1d miss rate target ≤2%; untiled 1024³ exhibits ~L2-bound thrashing (the headline tiling win below).

Roofline analysis

  • Per kernel compute arithmetic intensity (AI = FLOP/byte) → classify against host roofline (peak ≈ cores·SIMD_lanes·2·freq FLOP/s; mem BW ≈ STREAM-triad GB/s).
Kernel AI (FLOP/byte) Bound Optimization lever
elementwise Add/Relu ~0.08–0.17 memory vectorize + parallel; fusion (cut intermediate traffic)
Reduce sum ~0.25 memory vectorize + tree reduction; parallel partial sums
MatMul (untiled) low effective (cache miss) memory tile to lift AI into compute region
MatMul (tiled+packed) high (reuse) compute vectorize microkernel + unroll + parallel
Conv2d mid compute (tiled) tile over CHW; vectorize over C
  • Roofline plot emitted by tcc bench --roofline (CSV: AI, GFLOP/s, kernel name) — confirms memory-bound kernels sit on the BW ceiling, compute-bound near the SIMD ceiling.

The fusion win (dominant CPU optimization)

  • Fusion (Phase 4) eliminates intermediate materialization: an N-element elementwise chain of length L drops from L kernel launches + (L−1)·N intermediate read/write traffic to 1 kernel + 0 intermediate traffic.
  • For memory-bound chains, speedup ≈ traffic ratio. Conv2d → BiasAdd → Relu: unfused reads/writes the Conv2d output (e.g. 64×56×56 f32 = ~12.8 MB) three times across kernels; fused, the bias+relu run in-register on each conv output element before store → ≥1.6× (target), measured against the 3-kernel unfused path.
  • Reported as fusion_speedup = t_unfused / t_fused per fused group in tcc bench. Negative fusion (a fusion that hurts, e.g. fusing across a reduce that blocks vectorization) is gated by the cost model (§12) and flagged.

Single-kernel optimization decomposition (matmul, 1024³)

Cumulative GFLOP/s, each row adds one schedule primitive (illustrative shape of the curve, host-dependent):

Schedule Primitive added GFLOP/s % BLAS Why
naive ijk — ~2 ~3% K-loop innermost → B column stride misses
reorder ikj reorder ~4 ~6% unit-stride C/B inner
tiled tile (32×32×K-block) ~12 ~18% L1/L2 reuse, AI ↑
vectorized vectorize (8 lanes) ~22 ~33% AVX2 FMA
unrolled µkernel unroll ~28 ~42% hide FMA latency, fewer branches
parallel parallel (8 cores) ~120 (multi) scale outer M-tile over thread pool
  • Tiling for cache locality: tile factors chosen so each operand tile fits a cache level (L1 µkernel block, L2 panel); this is the lever that moves matmul off the memory ceiling.
  • Vectorize for SIMD: innermost axis → lane width (8× f32 AVX2). Codegen vectorization contract (Phase 8): vectorized axis must be contiguous in the Buffer; non-multiple extent → masked/remainder tail.
  • Parallel for cores: outer tile axis → thread pool; target ≥6× on 8 cores for compute-bound (≥0.75 parallel efficiency); memory-bound saturates BW at ~3–4 threads (report efficiency, do not over-thread).

Allocator & memory-plan analysis

  • MemoryPlan reuse ratio = sum(tensor_bytes) / peak_resident_bytes; target ≥2× on the model corpus (liveness reuse over topological order, §10). Reported by tcc dump module --mem.
  • Steady-state allocation: after first iteration the GraphExecutor performs 0 heap allocations per run — all buffers pre-planned, Allocator hands out slices of one pre-sized arena. Verified by a counting-allocator test asserting alloc_calls == 0 on iterations ≥2 (gate).
  • Zero-copy: NDArray reshape/slice/transpose produce strided views (no copy); benchmark asserts a Reshape of 16M f32 is O(1) (<1 µs) vs a materializing copy (~ms).

Dispatch-overhead analysis (executor vs VM)

Path Per-op overhead When it dominates
GraphExecutor (static) ~tens of ns (precomputed topo order, direct kernel ptr call) negligible above ~10K elem/op
tc-vm Interpreter (dynamic) ~hundreds of ns (instruction decode, register/object indirection, runtime alloc) tiny tensors, control-flow-heavy graphs
  • Crossover: for n ≤ ~1K elements/op, dispatch + alloc overhead can exceed compute → VM is 3–8× slower per op than the executor; for large tensors both converge (kernel-bound). Mitigation: static-shape subgraphs always routed to GraphExecutor; VM reserved for control flow / Dim::Dynamic.

Auto-tuner improvement (Phase 12)

  • Reported as gflops_tuned / gflops_default_schedule per fused kernel. Target: ≥1.5× over the heuristic default schedule on matmul/conv after a bounded search (≤512 trials, analytical cost model pre-prune → measure top-K). Search wall-time budget logged; convergence curve (best GFLOP/s vs trial) emitted to CSV.
  • Cost-model accuracy: rank-correlation (Spearman) of predicted vs measured ≥0.8 on held-out schedules; mispredictions bounded so the measured top-K contains the true best.

Tail behavior & pathological shapes

Pathology Effect Mitigation / report
tiny tensors (n<1K), many ops dispatch overhead ≫ compute fusion collapses chains; report dispatch-bound flag
non-power-of-2 / prime extents vectorize remainder tail, partial tiles masked tail loop; perf cliff noted, not crash
highly skewed shapes (1×N, N×1 matmul) degenerate tiles, BW-bound fall back to elementwise/gemv schedule
huge K, tiny M,N accumulator-bound, poor parallelism split K + parallel-reduce
dynamic shape via VM per-call alloc + no static plan arena pooling in Interpreter; report alloc count
  • Tail metrics: alongside median, report p99 run latency and worst-case shape in the corpus; a perf cliff is acceptable iff numerically correct (oracle still passes) — performance regressions are warnings, correctness failures are hard errors.

CI performance gates

  • Per-pass compile time: ±10% vs baseline → warn, ±25% → fail.
  • Kernel GFLOP/s: < target row (above) → fail; between target and stretch → pass with note.
  • alloc_calls == 0 steady-state: hard gate.
  • fusion_speedup ≥ 1.6 on the fused-conv fixture: hard gate.
  • All perf gates run on a pinned CI runner class; numbers normalized to a stored host-capability baseline to tolerate runner variance (±15% band).

Scalability Analysis

Scaling axes: compile-time (graph size, fusion, tuning), runtime (tensor size × batch, op count), parallelism (intra-kernel, inter-op), memory (MemoryPlan footprint, IR/Module size), and the auto-tuner search space. The compiler is single-threaded; the runtime is the parallel hot path. GPU/distributed are deferred (#248/#180, #351).

Compile-Time Scaling

Let N = Graph nodes, E = edges, G = fusion groups, K = emitted Kernels, P = schedule primitives/group, T = tuner trials.

Stage Crate Complexity Note
Import (ONNX-subset / textual) tc-graph O(N) one Arena alloc/node
Shape/type inference tc-pass O(N·I) → fixed point I = iterations; acyclic ⇒ I=1 (single topo pass), cyclic-symbolic ⇒ I≤ depth
Const fold / CSE / DCE / simplify tc-pass O(N) / O(N) hash / O(N) / O(N) CSE = value-numbering hashmap; near-linear
Layout transform tc-pass O(N) cost-aware insert, local decision/node
Fusion dominator analysis tc-fuse O(N·α(N)) ≈ near-linear dom-tree build (Lengauer-Tarjan) + group walk
Lower group → PrimFunc tc-ir O(G · ops/group) linear in IR node count
Schedule apply tc-schedule O(P · loop-nest size) per-group
Codegen (CBackend/SimdBackend) tc-codegen O(K · TIR size) string emission linear in Stmt/Expr count
cc invocation external O(K) processes dominant fixed-cost: ~50–200 ms/kernel cold
Auto-tune (Phase 12) tc-schedule O(G · T · measure) only superlinear/dominant term; bounded by budget
  • All graph passes + fusion + lowering + codegen are linear/near-linear in N. Target: ≤ 2 s to compile a ResNet-50-class graph (~250 ops → ~40 fused kernels) without tuning.
  • cc fork/exec dominates untuned compile wall-clock for large K. Mitigation: batch all Kernels into one C translation unit (single cc call), cache by content hash, --jobs parallel cc (the one place compile-side parallelism is allowed — external processes, not the pass pipeline).
  • The pass pipeline itself is single-threaded (stated invariant): deterministic ordering, no intra-compiler thread pool. Acceptable because passes are near-linear and cc/tuning are the real costs.

Compiler memory

Object Footprint Scaling
Graph (Arena<GraphNode>) ~64–128 B/node + args O(N)
PrimFunc/Stmt/Expr ~32–48 B/IR node O(IR size) ≈ O(N · avg ops/group)
Module (kernels + graph + plan) Σ Kernel.source bytes O(K · kernel src)

Peak compiler RSS target ≤ 1 GB for N ≤ 10⁴ nodes. Mitigation if exceeded: drop intermediate Graphs after lowering (keep only Module), arena reset between groups.

Runtime Scaling

Two regimes per op: compute-bound (FLOPs ≫ bytes) vs memory-bound (bytes ≫ FLOPs), set by arithmetic intensity AI = FLOPs/bytes vs the machine ridge point (roughly peak-GFLOP/s ÷ mem-GB/s).

Op class Cost model Scaling
MatMul [M,K]·[K,N] 2·M·N·K FLOPs cubic in dim, compute-bound at large K
Conv2d 2·N·Cout·Cin·H·W·Kh·Kw compute-bound; AI rises with channels
Elementwise (Add/Mul/Relu) O(elements), AI≈O(1) memory-bound ⇒ fusion-critical
Reduce O(elements), 1 pass memory-bound
  • Batch B: kernel FLOPs scale linearly in B for fused elementwise/conv; throughput (GFLOP/s) ~ flat once B saturates the parallel axis, latency ~ linear. Static B → GraphExecutor; dynamic B (Dim::Dynamic) → tc-vm allocates at runtime.
  • Dispatch overhead: per-op fixed cost (executor topo step / VM InvokeKernel) ≈ 50–500 ns. For tiny tensors this dominates ⇒ dispatch-bound. Mitigation: fusion (cuts K), and the VM amortizes via register reuse. Rule of thumb: ops with < ~10 µs kernel time are dispatch-sensitive.
  • GraphExecutor cost = Σ kernel time + O(K) dispatch; VM adds branch/Call overhead (control flow) but reuses the same compiled Kernels.

Parallelism

Level Mechanism Where Scaling
Intra-kernel Schedule::parallel(ax) → thread pool over tiles tc-schedule/tc-codegen ~linear speedup to core count, then Amdahl-bound by serial reduce/tail
Intra-kernel SIMD vectorize(ax) → SimdBackend lanes tc-codegen width × (4/8/16 for f32 under SSE/AVX2/AVX-512)
Inter-op async op dispatch (tc-runtime) tc-runtime overlaps independent branches of the dataflow DAG
Compiler none — single-threaded (except parallel cc) — by design
  • Strong scaling ceiling: number of physical cores; hyperthread gains marginal for compute-bound kernels. Parallel parallel-axis must have extent ≥ cores × ~4 for load balance; small extents → grain too coarse, mitigate by fuse+split to expose a larger parallel axis.
  • Inter-op async wins only when the DAG has width (parallel branches) and ops are large enough to hide dispatch/sync; serial chains gain nothing.

Memory Scaling (MemoryPlan)

  • Without reuse: peak = Σ all intermediate tensor bytes (O(N · tensor size)).
  • With liveness-based MemoryPlan: peak = max over topo cut of simultaneously-live tensors (register-allocation-on-tensors). Typical reduction 2–10× on deep feed-forward graphs (only ~2 buffers live in a linear chain).
peak_no_reuse  = Σ_v  bytes(v)
peak_planned   = max_t  Σ_{v live at t} bytes(v)
  • Fusion further shrinks footprint: fused intermediates never materialize (no Storage allocated).
  • Allocator: pooled, size-classed; alloc/free O(1) amortized; fragmentation bounded by reuse of identical-shape slots. Giant single tensors (> pool block) → direct mmap-style alloc.
  • Storage is ref-counted; zero-copy NDArray views (reshape/slice) add no bytes (share ptr+offset, new strides).

Auto-Tuner Search-Space Explosion (Phase 12)

Naïve space per fused kernel is combinatorial: tile factors × loop orders × vectorize/unroll/parallel choices.

|space| ≈ ∏_axis (#tile_factors)  ×  (#valid_reorders)  ×  2^(#unroll axes) × ...
        → 10^3 .. 10^7 candidates per kernel  (intractable to exhaust)

Bounding mechanisms:

  • Explicit trial budget T (per kernel, e.g. 64–512 measured trials) — hard cap on superlinear cost.
  • Cost model (analytical: FLOPs, AI, footprint, vector-width fit; + measured) prunes before measuring — only top-m candidates compiled+run.
  • Restrict tile factors to divisors / powers-of-two; legality filter drops invalid schedules early.
  • Result cached per (op-shape, Target) signature ⇒ amortized across identical kernels.

Total tune cost ≈ O(G · T · (codegen + cc + run)). This is the intended budget knob: untuned compile is seconds, full-tune is minutes–hours and explicitly opt-in via tcc tune.

Ceilings, Bottlenecks, Mitigations

Regime Bottleneck Symptom Mitigation
Huge graph (N ≫ 10⁴) tuning blow-up; cc count minutes/hours compile budget T; single TU + cached cc; tune hot kernels only
Many tiny ops dispatch overhead runtime ≫ kernel FLOPs aggressive fusion; lower K; VM register reuse
Giant tensors memory bandwidth low % of peak GFLOP/s tile for cache; layout transform; check AI vs ridge
Wide batch, small op parallel grain poor core scaling fuse axes → larger parallel extent
Deep linear chain peak footprint OOM risk MemoryPlan reuse + fusion
Dynamic shapes runtime alloc churn allocator pressure VM pooled Allocator; shape-bucketed kernel cache

Out of scope ceilings (deferred): GPU codegen (#248/#180) lifts the compute ceiling; distributed execution (#351) lifts the single-host memory/compute ceiling. Here: single CPU host, target ≥ 50% of an optimized BLAS on tuned MatMul and ≥ 5–10× over the naive nested-loop baseline on fused elementwise/conv, within f32 rtol 1e-5 / atol 1e-6.

Mental Models & First Principles

Six compressed expert insights — the load-bearing abstractions a principal engineer internalizes. Each: the insight, what it predicts, the bug class you write without it, and the § where this blueprint applies it.

MM1 — A PrimFunc is a pure mathematical specification; its schedule is an orthogonal optimization. Insight: TensorExpr defines what is computed (a mapping from output indices to a scalar expression); every legal Schedule is a different strategy for the same mathematical function. Predicts: you can explore thousands of loop orderings without ever re-verifying correctness — only one oracle comparison is needed per compute definition. Bug class without it: engineers hand-tune kernel order and break numerical results by changing reduction grouping; or they conflate "correct loop nest" with "optimal loop nest" and never search. Applied: §8.3 compute/schedule worked example; §11.6 schedule-primitive contract; §6.2 P2 invariant; Phase 7–8 (§19–20).

MM2 — Operator fusion is a memory-traffic problem, not a compute problem. Insight: DL workloads on CPU are predominantly memory-bound for elementwise and reduce ops (arithmetic intensity ≈ O(1)); fusing eliminates intermediate tensor round-trips to DRAM, not FLOPs. Predicts: a 4-op unfused elementwise chain reads/writes the full tensor 8 times (4 reads + 4 writes); fused, it reads once and writes once — an immediate 8× BW reduction independent of compute. Bug class without it: optimizing a memory-bound kernel for throughput by adding vectorization while leaving intermediate tensors materialized, achieving negligible real-world speedup. Applied: §8.5 fusion analysis; §6.5 P5 "fusion eliminates materialization"; Phase 4 (§16) fusion rationale; §4.3 kernel performance targets.

MM3 — Buffer liveness is register allocation on tensors. Insight: the liveness analysis that decides which intermediate tensors can share storage is structurally identical to register allocation in a scalar compiler — live intervals that do not overlap can alias the same storage slot. Predicts: the peak memory requirement equals the maximum simultaneously-live byte count (the "register pressure"), not the sum of all intermediate sizes. Bug class without it: allocating a fresh Storage per intermediate tensor, which causes 2–10× peak memory over the minimum achievable; or reusing a storage slot for two overlapping live ranges, causing a WAR/WAW hazard. Applied: §8.7 MemoryPlan worked example; §7.6 INV-5; §11.8 MemoryPlan; Phase 10 (§22).

MM4 — Two IRs exist because graph-level and loop-level invariants are incompatible. Insight: the Graph IR (whole-tensor dataflow) enforces SSA-like value immutability and type-propagation invariants; TIR (loop-nest) enforces buffer-binding, index-affinity, and scope legality. Mixing them forces every pass to defend both invariant sets simultaneously — the layering cost of a single unified IR. Predicts: passes operating on the wrong IR level are necessarily more complex (a tiling pass on a graph IR must understand TensorType; a shape-inference pass on TIR must understand loop bounds). Bug class without it: CSE implemented on raw TIR instead of the graph — must handle Load/Store aliasing to find equivalent expressions, missing cross-op redundancies that graph-level CSE finds trivially. Applied: §6.3 P3; §8.1–§8.2 IR designs; §3.2 per-box responsibilities; ADR-03.

MM5 — The KernelSig is the entire trust boundary between compiler and runtime. Insight: once a Kernel is compiled to a .so, the only information the runtime can rely on is the KernelSig — pointer order, dtype, rank, and stride layout. The compiled binary is opaque; any discrepancy between KernelSig at the codegen side and at the load side is an ABI bug producing silent memory corruption, not a compile error. Predicts: any change to how CBackend orders parameters (e.g., inputs before outputs vs. interleaved) that is not reflected in the loaded KernelSig causes the executor to pass the wrong pointer as the first argument — wrong result or segfault. Bug class without it: the backend is refactored to reorder buffer arguments for performance reasons, the runtime is not updated, and the mismatch goes undetected until a model produces garbage output on a specific shape. Applied: §6.8 P8; §7.8 INV-7; §11.7 KernelSig; Phase 9 (§21) round-trip test; ADR-07.

MM6 — Determinism requires a fixed parallel-reduction tree, not just a fixed schedule. Insight: IEEE-754 floating-point addition is not associative; parallel reduction (parallel(k) over a reduce axis) changes summation order across threads, producing results that differ from the serial reference by up to O(N·ε) accumulated error. Determinism requires the reduction tree to be fixed (split factor determines the exact partial-sum grouping) so the same floating-point operations happen in the same order every run. Predicts: a thread-pool over a reduce axis that uses work-stealing (dynamic partition) will produce different results across runs due to non-deterministic scheduling — a floating-point non-determinism bug that appears only under load. Bug class without it: tuned reduction kernels pass correctness tests in serial CI but produce flaky tcc check failures under multi-core deployment. Applied: §4.7 determinism; §6.9 P9; §7.3 INV-2 "subtleties"; Phase 8 (§20) parallel on reduce; ADR-17.

Comparative Architecture Table

This design vs five production tensor compilation systems across the load-bearing architectural dimensions.

Dimension This design (tc-compiler) Apache TVM (Relay/TIR) Google XLA / HLO Halide MLIR (linalg/vector dialects) ONNX Runtime (CPU EP)
Graph IR Relay-style typed dataflow DAG (tc-graph); functional, SSA-like, arena+typed-ids Relay: functional dataflow, type system, ADT support for control flow HLO: ONNX-adjacent purely functional ops; no user-visible control flow Compute functions (Func/RDom) — no explicit graph IR; pipelines are schedules of stages linalg named ops on memrefs; fully generalized op dialect system ONNX graph; flat node list, attribute-driven
Loop IR TIR (tc-ir): For/Store/Expr loop nest; buffer-scoped; schedule-orthogonal TIR: near-identical; IterVar kinds (Data/Reduce/Thread/etc.); rich IterVarRelation graph Thunk/emitter generates LLVM IR directly from HLO; no explicit loop IR No IR — Func defines compute, Halide JIT compiles to LLVM; schedule = IR rewrite Affine/vector/SCF dialects; polyhedral analysis built-in Graph-level only; operators call vendor kernels
Codegen backend C source via CBackend + SimdBackend (portable SIMD); optional LLVM feature-gated TVM: LLVM primary; C/CUDA/Metal/Vulkan backends; WebAssembly XLA: LLVM (CPU), custom HLO→CUDA compiler (GPU) Halide: LLVM; target-specific intrinsics for AVX2/NEON/CUDA MLIR: LLVM translation pass; target-specific lowering passes Vendor kernels (oneDNN/OpenBLAS/Eigen); no codegen
Compute/schedule separation Explicit (Halide model): TensorExpr = WHAT; Schedule = HOW; invariant enforced Same as Halide for TIR/TE; Relay higher-level is schedule-free No explicit user schedule; fusion and tiling are compiler passes over HLO Origin of the model; compute (Func) and schedule (Pipeline) are first-class Partially: linalg is compute-only; tiling/vectorization are transformation passes None — vendor kernels are black boxes
Fusion strategy Dominator-tree grouping (elementwise/injective + ≤1 reduce) TVM: pattern-based + dominator rules; FuseOps pass XLA: elemental/reduce/fusion policies in passes; "fusion cost model" Pipeline-local producer-consumer inlining; compute_at moves producer into consumer Tile-and-fuse transformation; linalg.fuse_elementwise_ops Static kernel-level; no inter-op fusion on CPU
Memory planning Liveness-based MemoryPlan (register-allocation-on-tensors); stored in Module TVM: buffer allocation pass over topo order (similar liveness model) XLA: buffer assignment as a dedicated pass; handles aliasing, memory space Implicit; Halide manages intermediate storage per compute_at placement Allocation pass; buffer reuse via bufferization dialect Preallocated arena; no liveness-based reuse
Dynamic shapes Dim::Dynamic in Graph IR; route to tc-vm bytecode interpreter Relay VM handles dynamic shapes; AOT for static, VM for dynamic XLA requires static shapes; dynamic handled via tf.function tracing Static at compile time (Halide JIT); no runtime dynamic dispatch ShapedType::kDynamic; shape inference passes handle dynamic dims ONNX supports dynamic dims; runtime resolves at each step
Auto-tuning Analytical + measured cost model; budget-bounded search (Phase 12) AutoTVM (template+ML cost model), Ansor (hierarchical search) XLA Autotuner (ROCm/CUDA); less exposed on CPU Autoscheduler (Halide 2019) based on beam search over tiling/compute_at No built-in tuner; external tools (e.g., IREE) provide tuning No tuning; vendor kernels are pre-tuned
Verification / oracle Per-pass verifier (typed IR); external NumPy-style oracle; differential fuzzing Per-pass type checker; conformance tests vs reference Numerical tests vs TF eager reference Halide's generate/verify framework MLIR verifier (op-level); unit tests per dialect ONNX conformance test suite

Apache TVM. Made the same Relay/TIR two-IR split but chose LLVM as the primary codegen target (vs C+portable-SIMD here), accepting a large build dependency in exchange for tighter per-target optimization (vectorization via LLVM's loop vectorizer, precise target feature modeling). TVM wins in production environments where LLVM is already the system compiler and the marginal cost of the LLVM dep is zero; its AutoTVM/Ansor tuners are also more sophisticated (learned cost models, evolutionary search). This blueprint rejects it as default because the C-source path is more portable, debuggable (readable emitted kernels), and avoids the LLVM version-pinning problem on constrained CI environments.

Google XLA / HLO. Chose a flat, purely functional op set (HLO) with no user-visible schedule abstraction — all tiling, fusion, and layout are compiler-internal decisions made by XLA passes, not exposed to the programmer. XLA wins for TensorFlow/JAX deployment where the user never interacts with schedules and the compiler must make globally optimal decisions automatically. The cost is opacity: debugging a slow HLO kernel requires instrumenting XLA internals. This blueprint rejects the implicit-schedule model because the explicit schedule space (a learning objective) and the auto-tuner (Phase 12) only make sense if schedules are first-class.

Halide. Origin of the compute/schedule separation this blueprint adopts directly. Halide operates without an explicit graph IR — each Func is a compute stage, and the schedule is expressed directly on those stages. It wins for image-processing pipelines where the dataflow is a fixed producer-consumer chain and domain experts want direct schedule control. For a multi-op DL workload with a variable topology (imported from ONNX), the absence of a graph IR means there is no place to do graph-level passes (CSE, DCE, fusion rule analysis over the full DAG). This blueprint adds the graph IR layer on top of the Halide schedule model.

MLIR (linalg/vector dialects). Chose a dialect-extensible multi-level IR with a uniform Op/Type/Attribute system, enabling arbitrary new IR nodes without forking the compiler. Wins in a research or production infrastructure where many hardware backends (TPU, GPU variants, novel NPUs) each need custom dialects and passes, and where sharing the infrastructure cost across targets is the goal. The MLIR op-definition machinery (TableGen, OpDSL) is a multi-year investment — appropriate for a compiler infrastructure team, not for a from-scratch single-owner compiler that needs to be buildable and teachable in 32 weeks.

ONNX Runtime (CPU EP). Chose not to generate kernels at all: instead delegates to vendor libraries (oneDNN, OpenBLAS, Eigen) for every op. Wins in a production inference service where peak throughput on known hardware is the only objective and the engineering team cannot afford to maintain a codegen stack. The cost is that the system cannot tune for new hardware, cannot fuse cross-op, and cannot teach the user anything about scheduling or code generation — the exact skills this blueprint is designed to build.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All numbers derived step-by-step from §4 System Constraints. Baseline hardware: x86_64, AVX2, 8 cores, ~50 GB/s DRAM BW, ~3 GHz, ~256 GFLOP/s peak f32 AVX2 FMA (8 cores × 2 FMA/cycle × 8 lanes × 3 GHz).

N1 — Fused elementwise throughput (memory-bandwidth ceiling)

From §4.3: target ≥ 80% of measured DRAM BW for fused elementwise.

BW_peak = 50 GB/s
BW_target = 0.80 × 50 = 40 GB/s
Bytes_per_element (f32) = 4 B
Elements/sec = 40e9 / 4 = 10e9 = 10 GELEM/s
For a length-N fused add+relu: time = N × 4 B / 40e9
  N=1e8 elements → time = 1e8 × 4 / 40e9 = 10 ms

Implication: a fused 100M-element chain executes in ~10 ms; an unfused 4-op chain (4× memory passes) takes ~40 ms — 4× slower.

N2 — Unfused vs fused intermediate buffer bytes (fusion win)

From §8.5 Conv2d→Add→Relu example and §4.4 MemoryPlan target.

Conv2d output: f32[1,64,112,112] = 64 × 112 × 112 × 4 B = 3.21 MB
Add intermediate (bias): same shape = 3.21 MB
Total intermediate materialization (unfused) = 2 × 3.21 = 6.42 MB
Fused: 0 MB (accumulator lives in registers/Local scope)
DRAM round-trip savings: 6.42 MB read + 6.42 MB written = 12.84 MB per inference pass
At 40 GB/s: time saved = 12.84e6 / 40e9 = 0.32 ms per conv-add-relu group

N3 — MemoryPlan peak memory reduction

From §4.4: target ≥ 40% reduction vs no-reuse on MLP/conv corpus. From §8.7 worked example.

5-intermediate graph (§8.7): naive = 4+4+2+2+4 = 16 MB; planned = 10 MB
Reduction = (16−10)/16 = 37.5%  ≈ meets the 40% target on a chain
Deep linear chain (10 nodes, each 4 MB): naive = 40 MB
  Only 2 simultaneously live at any step → planned = 8 MB
  Reduction = (40−8)/40 = 80%

N4 — Scheduled MatMul GFLOP/s target derivation

From §4.3: target ≥ 10× naive baseline; ≥ 8 GFLOP/s single-thread vectorized.

Peak AVX2 f32 FMA throughput (1 core, 3 GHz):
  2 FMA/cycle (fused multiply-add counts 2 FLOPs) × 8 lanes × 3e9 = 48 GFLOP/s/core theoretical
Cache-blocking efficiency estimate: 60% of peak (register pressure, load imbalance) → ~29 GFLOP/s
Vectorize + tile target: 8 GFLOP/s = 8/48 = 17% of peak (conservative: includes overhead)
Naive triple-loop (no tiling, poor cache): ~0.3–0.5 GFLOP/s (measured baseline)
10× over naive baseline: 0.4 × 10 = 4 GFLOP/s — below the 8 GFLOP/s single-thread target
  → The 8 GFLOP/s target is the binding constraint (requires proper tiling + AVX2 FMA)

N5 — Compile-time cc invocation cost

From §4.2: per-kernel cc compile target < 400 ms; ResNet-class in < 30 s.

ResNet-50 has ~50 conv ops + biases + activations → after fusion: ~15–20 fused groups → ~15–20 kernels
Serial cc cost: 20 kernels × 200 ms/kernel = 4 s  (within the 30 s budget)
Parallel cc (8 cores): 20/8 kernels in flight = ceil(20/8)=3 batches × 200 ms = 0.6 s
Single TU approach (all kernels in one file): 1 cc call × ~2–4 s = 2–4 s (vs 4 s serial)
  → parallel cc preferred for > 8 kernels; single TU preferred for < 4 kernels

N6 — Auto-tuner trial budget and wall-clock bound

From §4.2: tune budget ≤ 200 trials / ≤ 30 s per fused kernel.

Each measured trial: codegen (~10 ms) + cc (~200 ms) + kernel run (~5 ms) = ~215 ms
200 trials × 215 ms = 43 s  (exceeds 30 s limit by ~43%)
  → must prune: analytical cost model eliminates ~70% of candidates before measuring
200 × 0.30 = 60 measured trials × 215 ms = 12.9 s  (within 30 s budget)
For a large graph with 20 fused kernels: 20 × 12.9 s = 258 s ≈ 4.3 min full tune

N7 — Dispatch overhead fraction on small tensors

From §4.5: GraphExecutor per-op dispatch overhead < 1 µs/op.

Kernel work for a tiny tensor f32[64]: 64 × 4 B / 40 GB/s = 6.4 µs (memory-bound)
Dispatch overhead = 1 µs / 6.4 µs = 15.6% overhead → acceptable
Kernel work for f32[16]: 16 × 4 / 40e9 = 1.6 µs; dispatch = 1/1.6 = 62.5% → dispatch-bound
  → tensors below ~1000 elements are dispatch-dominated; fusion is critical to reduce K

N8 — Compiler RSS for a large graph

From §4.4: compiler peak RSS < 256 MB.

Arena<GraphNode> at ~128 B/node, N=10⁴: 10⁴ × 128 = 1.28 MB
TIR PrimFunc/Stmt/Expr at ~48 B/IR node, ~10 IR nodes/graph node:
  10⁴ × 10 × 48 = 4.8 MB
Module: 20 kernels × ~50 KB C source = 1 MB
Total ≈ 7 MB — well within 256 MB ceiling even with 10× slack
  At N=10⁵ (above stated max): ~70 MB — still within budget; pass pipeline stays sub-second

N9 — Parallel speedup ceiling (Amdahl)

From §4.3: parallel axis speedup ≥ 0.7·nproc on compute-bound kernels.

nproc = 8, target speedup ≥ 0.7 × 8 = 5.6×
Amdahl's law: speedup = 1 / (s + (1-s)/n), target 5.6× with n=8
  5.6 = 1 / (s + (1-s)/8) → s + (1-s)/8 = 1/5.6 = 0.179 → s(1 - 1/8) = 0.179 - 0.125 → s = 0.077
  → serial fraction must be < 7.7% (tail loops, reductions) for the 5.6× target
Loop tail (from split with factor 64 on extent 1000): tail = 1000%64 = 40 iters / 1000 = 4% serial
  → within budget

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

Fifteen staff-level questions the reader must be able to answer after studying this blueprint. Each cites the resolving §; each requires synthesizing at least two sections.

HQ1. A graph contains Conv2d → Add → Relu where the Add has a second input from an earlier Reshape. Does FusionPass fuse the full chain? Explain using the dominator boundary rule and state exactly which intermediate buffers are materialized and which are not. (Resolves: §8.5 fusion dominator analysis + §6.5 P5 + §7.7 INV-6)

HQ2. A user applies s.vectorize(j_inner) to the innermost axis of a MatMul schedule, but j_inner has a compile-time extent of 65 (not a multiple of 8 for AVX2). What does tc-schedule emit, and what does CBackend/SimdBackend produce for the tail? Trace the legality check through §8.3 and §11.6. (Resolves: §8.3 L1 + §8.4 + §11.6 Schedule primitives + §6.2 P2 schedule-invariance)

HQ3. The oracle comparison for a scheduled MatMul with parallel(i_outer) passes on N=512 but fails (max_rel_err=2.1e-4) on N=2048. What is the structural cause, how does P9 + INV-2 frame it, and what schedule change makes it deterministic? (Resolves: §4.7 determinism + §6.9 P9 + §7.3 INV-2 subtleties + §8.3 schedule primitives)

HQ4. You have a graph with 12 intermediate tensors (each 4 MB f32). After FusionPass, 4 fusion groups remain, each with 3 intermediates eliminated. Draw the liveness intervals for the remaining 0 materialized intermediates plus input/output tensors, compute MemoryPlan peak allocation, and compare to the §8.7 worked example. (Resolves: §8.7 MemoryPlan + §7.6 INV-5 + §4.4 memory constraints + §6.5 P5)

HQ5. A model has Dim::Dynamic("N") on the batch axis of its first MatMul. Trace the path from import through shape inference, fusion-group formation, TIR lowering, Module generation, and runtime execution, identifying at each stage what changes compared to the fully-static path. (Resolves: §8.1 Shape/§8.6 shape inference + §8.9 end-to-end sequence + §6.6 P6 + §11.9 tc-vm)

HQ6. SimdBackend emits a kernel, the CBackend emits the same kernel. Both pass tcc check on a f32[1024] input but SimdBackend fails on f32[1000]. Diagnose the failure at the KernelSig and loop-structure level, explain what the tail-handling invariant requires, and state which Phase and invariant gate this. (Resolves: §6.8 P8 + §7.8 INV-7 + §11.7 KernelSig + §8.3 L1 legality + §4.1 tolerance)

HQ7. The PassManager runs ConstFold then CSE then DCE then AlgebraicSimplify. A developer proposes running DCE before CSE to reduce the CSE hashtable size. Is this safe per P4 and INV-4? What analysis does each pass declare as invalidates(), and would reordering change the final graph? (Resolves: §6.4 P4 + §7.5 INV-4 + §11.4 PassManager + Phase 3 §15)

HQ8. You need to tune a Conv2d kernel on a machine with AVX-512 (16-lane f32). What changes in the Target, SimdBackend, and Schedule compared to the AVX2 example in §8.3? Compute the new vectorized GFLOP/s ceiling using N5 from the Capacity Model. (Resolves: §8.3 + §11.7 Target + §4.3 perf targets + N4/N5 capacity model + ADR-09)

HQ9. MemoryPlan assigns t3 and t4 to the same storage slot S2 in the §8.7 example. Async dispatch (§7.10 INV-9) allows independent ops to run concurrently. Under what condition would t3's consumer and t4's producer overlap in execution, and what contract does the runtime enforce to prevent the aliasing hazard? (Resolves: §7.6 INV-5 + §7.10 INV-9 + §6.7 P7 + §11.8 GraphExecutor async)

HQ10. The auto-tuner in Phase 12 explores tile factors for a MatMul [M=1024, K=1024, N=1024]. The search space is all divisors of 1024 for each of three tile axes. Use N6 from the Capacity Model to compute the number of candidates, estimate the unbudgeted measurement cost, and explain how the analytical cost model reduces it to within the 30 s budget. (Resolves: Scalability Analysis auto-tuner search-space section + N6 capacity model + §6.11 P11 + Phase 12 §24)

HQ11. A user reports that tcc compile produces a Module that passes tcc check but tcc bench shows 0.8 GFLOP/s on a MatMul when the target is ≥ 8 GFLOP/s. Without looking at the schedule, identify the three most likely schedule-level causes (from §8.3 and §8.4), the diagnostic commands (tcc dump) that expose each, and the schedule fix. (Resolves: §8.3 worked MatMul + §8.4 primitives + §6.12 P12 + §4.3 perf targets + N4 capacity)

HQ12. Explain why the tc-graph and tc-ir crates are no_std + alloc while tc-codegen is std. Then explain what changes if a user embeds the compiler in a bare-metal RTOS that has alloc but no OS-level cc invocation. Which ADR rows cover this and what path remains viable? (Resolves: §1.5 crate set + §4.9 platform constraints + ADR-07 + ADR-18 + §11.7 CodeGen trait)

HQ13. Walk through the full INV-2 violation scenario: vectorize is applied to a Reduce axis without the deterministic-tree flag. Trace the bug from Schedule::vectorize (§11.6) through CBackend emission, kernel execution, tcc check comparison, and identify which telemetry field first signals the violation. (Resolves: §7.3 INV-2 + §11.6 Schedule reduce safety + §6.9 P9 + §4.1 oracle contract + §7.13 invariant matrix)

HQ14. A FusionGroup contains 300 nodes (above the 256-node cap in §4.6). The FusionPass must split it. Describe the splitting strategy (using dominator boundary semantics from §8.5), state which intermediate tensors must now materialize, and quantify the memory impact using N2 from the Capacity Model. (Resolves: §4.6 scale assumptions + §8.5 fusion boundary + §6.5 P5 + N2 + §7.7 INV-6)

HQ15. The tc-vm Interpreter dispatches InvokeKernel for a kernel compiled for f32[batch, 512] but the runtime register holds an NDArray of shape f32[batch, 256]. Trace the runtime path through INV-10, identify the VmTrap variant, and explain why this is a trap (not UB) — and contrast with what would happen in the GraphExecutor if the MemoryPlan were built for the wrong static shape. (Resolves: §7.11 INV-10 + §11.9 VmInstruction + §6.10 P10 + §7.6 INV-5 static executor hazard)

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Four fundamentally different architectures for a tensor compiler + runtime, each representing a different design space.

Alt-1 — Single unified IR (no two-level split)

model
  |-- import
  v
Unified IR (ops carry loop/schedule annotations inline)
  |-- fusion/tiling/vectorize passes (all on same IR)
  v
codegen  -->  Module  -->  runtime

Regime where it wins: a toy compiler targeting a single hardware accelerator with a fixed ISA where the distinction between "graph-level" and "loop-level" is irrelevant (e.g., a matrix engine that operates on fixed 16×16 tiles; there are no loops to tile). A single IR with op-level tiling attributes requires zero lowering overhead. Real system embodying it: ONNX Runtime's operator-level representation for fixed-shape inference — each node maps directly to a kernel, no loop structure exposed. Why this blueprint rejected it: the compute/schedule separation is the central architectural thesis (§6.2 P2). A unified IR forces tiling attributes into every GraphOp, making graph-level CSE and DCE either impossible (must also CSE loop structures) or require layer-stripping anyway — reconstructing the two-IR split implicitly.

Alt-2 — Interpretation-only (no AOT codegen)

model
  |-- import
  v
Graph IR  -->  pass + fusion  -->  Interpreted executor
                                   (eval each op with reference kernel)

Regime where it wins: a research tool where compile time dominates experimentation time and the workload changes every run (e.g., NAS search that generates thousands of unique model topologies per hour). Compiling each model to native code is counterproductive; a fast interpreter backed by pre-compiled op libraries (like PyTorch eager mode) wins. Real system embodying it: TensorFlow 2.x eager execution, PyTorch eager mode — no compilation, each op dispatched to a pre-compiled kernel immediately. Why this blueprint rejected it: the entire goal is to learn and build the codegen + schedule pipeline. An interpretation-only architecture sidesteps the fusion pass (no materialized loop structure to fuse into), the schedule space (no loop axes to tile/vectorize), and the codegen backend — eliminating the three most instruction-rich components of the project.

Alt-3 — Polyhedral compiler (Pluto/isl-based tiling legality)

model
  |-- import
  v
Polyhedral IR (affine loop nests with dependence polyhedra)
  |-- Pluto/isl tiling, fusion, parallelization (automatic)
  v
LLVM IR  -->  Module  -->  runtime

Regime where it wins: scientific computing kernels (stencils, iterative solvers, structured meshes) where data-dependence patterns are statically provable and the polyhedral model can automatically find the optimal tiling without a search budget. Pluto's automatic compute_at is provably optimal for affine loop nests with bounded trip counts. Real system embodying it: Tensor Comprehensions (Vasilache et al., 2018, P8) and Polly (LLVM's polyhedral optimizer). Why this blueprint rejected it: the polyhedral model requires affine index expressions throughout — Dim::Dynamic symbolic dims immediately escape the polyhedral domain. More critically, the polyhedral framework (isl or Omega) is a large external dependency (comparable in complexity to LLVM itself) and replaces rather than teaches the schedule-primitive intuition that is the learning goal of Phases 7–8.

Alt-4 — Ahead-of-time kernel library (template metaprogramming)

op registry (e.g. Conv2d stride=1 pad=0, f32)
  |-- select pre-written kernel template
  |-- specialize at compile time (C++ template instantiation)
  v
specialized kernel .so  -->  Module  -->  runtime

Regime where it wins: production inference on a fixed set of known model architectures (e.g., ResNet-50, BERT-base, YOLOv5) where all op shapes are known at library build time and vendor-optimized templates outperform any auto-generated kernel. NVIDIA's cuDNN, Intel's oneDNN, and Arm Compute Library all follow this model. Real system embodying it: ONNX Runtime's CPU Execution Provider with oneDNN; every Conv2d is dispatched to a pre-optimized MKL/oneDNN kernel. Why this blueprint rejected it: the template library approach scales as O(ops × targets × shapes) in implementation effort, requires human experts for each new architecture, and cannot learn — the entire auto-tuning (Phase 12) and schedule-search premise is that the compiler can discover better schedules than a human would write. A template library also teaches nothing about IR design, schedule primitives, or codegen.

Deferred Features Register

Features deferred out of this blueprint's scope. Each entry: gate anchor, resolving project or future phase, and the enum variant / stub that holds the slot.

Deferred Feature Gate Anchor Resolving Scope Stub in Codebase
GPU / CUDA codegen PhaseDeferred("FINAL_BLUEPRINT §1.2") Projects #248 / #180 Device::Gpu, Scope::Shared, IterKind::Thread — compile but unimplemented!()
Full ONNX op set (beyond documented subset) PhaseDeferred("Phase 1 §13") Future op-set expansion Importer returns Err(UnknownOp) on out-of-subset ops
Distributed execution PhaseDeferred("→ #351") Project #351 Single-process architecture; no network transport types
Ansor-style learned cost model / evolutionary search PhaseDeferred("Phase 12 §24") Phase 12 extension Analytical + measured baseline; learned model not wired
LLVM codegen path Feature-gated (llvm cargo feature, off by default) Optional build flag CBackend always available; LLVM path behind #[cfg(feature = "llvm")]
Python frontend / pybind PhaseDeferred("NON-GOAL §1.2") Not in scope Textual graph format + ONNX-subset importer are the only frontends
F16 / BF16 / quantized dtypes PhaseDeferred("ADR-16") Future DType expansion DType enum has no F16/BF16 variants; I8 is present for integer ops

Glossary

Terms in dependency order where possible; each entry = precise, implementation-relevant definition tied to the locked spine. Type names link to their crate home.

Term Definition
graph IR High-level functional dataflow IR (Relay-like) in tc-graph. Whole-tensor ops, no loops. Graph { nodes: Arena<GraphNode>, inputs, outputs }; nodes carry GraphOp + Option<TensorType>. SSA-like, immutable post-build, arena + NodeId.
tensor IR (TIR) Low-level imperative loop-nest IR (TIR-like) in tc-ir. PrimFunc { params: Vec<Buffer>, body: Stmt }; explicit For/Store/Load over Buffers. One PrimFunc per fused group.
operator A whole-tensor op node in the graph IR: a GraphOp variant (Add, MatMul, Conv2d, Relu, Reduce(..), Call(name), …). Op registry is extensible by name.
dataflow graph The DAG formed by GraphNode.args: Vec<NodeId> edges. Topological order = execution order. No cycles (control flow lives in tc-vm, not the graph).
tensor expression TensorExpr (tc-ir): declarative "what is computed" per output element — the compute definition lowered from a fused GraphOp group, independent of loop order (Halide compute/schedule split).
compute/schedule separation Halide model: TensorExpr = WHAT; Schedule = HOW (loop order, tiling, vectorize, parallel). Invariant: schedule changes alter performance only, never the numerical result.
schedule primitive A loop-transform op on Schedule (tc-schedule): split, reorder, fuse, tile, vectorize, unroll, parallel, compute_at, inline. Each rewrites the Stage loop structure.
tiling Schedule::tile(ax, ay, fx, fy) — block two axes into fx×fy tiles (split + reorder composite) for cache locality. Tile sizes are tuning knobs (Phase 12).
loop reorder Schedule::reorder(&[AxisId]) — permute loop nesting order; e.g. push the reduce axis inward for vectorizable inner loops. Must preserve a valid dependency order.
split/fuse split(ax, factor) -> (outer, inner) divides one axis into two; fuse(a, b) -> AxisId collapses two adjacent axes into one. Inverse operations; split precedes vectorize/unroll/parallel.
vectorize Schedule::vectorize(ax) — map an axis to SIMD lanes; the inner extent should equal/divide the lane count. Codegen contract: SimdBackend emits portable-SIMD over that axis.
unroll Schedule::unroll(ax) — fully expand a (small, static-extent) loop into straight-line code; reduces branch overhead, enables scalar opt.
parallelize Schedule::parallel(ax) — map an axis to a thread pool; codegen partitions the axis into tiles per worker. Axis must be free of cross-iteration deps (not a reduce axis).
compute_at Schedule::compute_at(stage, ax) — compute a producer stage inside a consumer loop at axis ax, shrinking intermediate buffers (locality vs recompute trade-off).
inline Schedule::inline(stage) — substitute a producer's TensorExpr directly into consumers, eliminating its buffer entirely (the elementwise default).
operator fusion tc-fuse: merge an injective/elementwise chain (+ ≤1 reduce) into a single PrimFunc, removing intermediate tensor materialization. The dominant CPU win.
fusion group FusionGroup — the set of graph nodes selected to lower into one fused PrimFunc. Produced by FusionPass.
dominator analysis The fusion grouping algorithm: build the dominator tree of the dataflow graph; a node fuses upward into its immediate dominator's group when fusion rules permit (output reused only within the group).
shape inference tc-pass analysis propagating TensorType.shape through the graph to a fixed point. Resolves static dims; leaves Dim::Dynamic(name) symbolic for runtime.
type inference tc-pass analysis assigning each node a TensorType { shape, dtype }; the verifier asserts every GraphNode.ty is Some post-pass.
dynamic shape Dim::Dynamic(String) — a symbolic dimension unresolved at compile time; materialized at runtime by the tc-vm Interpreter (allocate-on-demand). GraphExecutor requires fully static shapes.
layout transform Phase 5 pass: rewrite data format (NCHW↔NHWC) + insert layout-convert nodes, cost-aware, to give kernels contiguous/vectorizable access.
canonicalization Normalizing IR to a single form (commutative operand order, layout, constant position) so later passes (CSE, fusion) match structurally.
constant folding tc-pass: evaluate subgraphs with all-Const inputs at compile time, replacing them with a Const(TensorData) node.
CSE Common-subexpression elimination: deduplicate structurally-identical nodes (post-canonicalization) into one, rewiring args.
DCE Dead-code elimination: drop nodes not reachable backward from Graph.outputs.
algebraic simplification Rewrite rules: x+0→x, x*1→x, x*0→0, transpose∘transpose→id, reduce/identity folding — applied with canonicalization.
pass manager PassManager (tc-pass): orders + runs Pass instances (analysis + transform), tracks invalidated analyses, drives to fixed point where required.
lowering Translating a higher IR to a lower one: graph fusion group → TensorExpr → naive PrimFunc (tc-ir); and scheduled TIR → target source (tc-codegen).
codegen tc-codegen: CodeGen::emit(&Schedule, &Target) -> Kernel. CBackend emits C source; SimdBackend emits portable-SIMD.
kernel Kernel { name, source, signature: KernelSig } — one emitted C function realizing one fused PrimFunc.
KernelSig The stable ABI of a Kernel: ordered pointer params + shape + stride params (no implicit globals), so the runtime can bind NDArrays positionally.
module Module { kernels: Vec<Kernel>, graph: Graph, plan: MemoryPlan } — the compiled artifact loaded by the runtime / VM.
NDArray tc-runtime tensor: { storage: Storage, shape, strides, dtype, offset } — a strided view; reshape/slice are zero-copy.
strided view An NDArray whose strides need not be contiguous; supports zero-copy transpose/slice by adjusting strides/offset without moving bytes.
zero-copy Producing a new view (reshape, slice, transpose) that shares the underlying Storage — no allocation, no copy.
storage Storage { ptr, bytes, device } — a ref-counted raw buffer; multiple NDArray views may share one Storage.
allocator Allocator trait: alloc(bytes) -> Storage / free(Storage). Backs runtime buffer acquisition; pluggable (bump/pool).
memory planning MemoryPlan — compile-time assignment of which tensors share Storage, computed from buffer liveness over the executor topological order (register-allocation-on-tensors).
buffer liveness The interval each intermediate tensor is live (first-def → last-use) in topological order; non-overlapping intervals may reuse the same Storage.
graph executor GraphExecutor (tc-runtime): dispatches Module.kernels in topological order over NDArray bindings. Static shapes only.
bytecode VM tc-vm Interpreter: executes VmFunc { instrs: Vec<VmInstruction>, num_regs } for dynamic control flow + dynamic shapes, dispatching the same compiled Kernels.
register A slot in the VM register file (RegId) holding an NDArray or object; VmInstructions read/write registers (LoadConst, AllocTensor, InvokeKernel, …).
closure Closure (tc-vm): a VmFunc reference + captured registers, enabling first-class function values / dynamic dispatch in the VM.
device Device enum (Cpu, Gpu deferred). Tags Storage placement; the runtime dispatches kernels per device (CPU-only in scope).
async dispatch Runtime scheduling of independent ops/kernels concurrently (thread pool); ordering constrained only by dataflow deps + buffer liveness.
cost model Phase 12: analytical + measured estimator scoring a candidate Schedule (FLOPs, cache footprint, vector utilization) to guide tuning without running every point.
auto-tuning Phase 12 search over the schedule primitive space (tile sizes, reorder, unroll factors) per fused kernel; selects the best Schedule via the cost model + measurement.
numerical tolerance The accepted deviation of compiled output from the oracle: f32 rtol 1e-5, atol 1e-6 (documented per dtype). Schedule changes must not exceed it.
oracle The NumPy-style reference implementation per op/model. Phase 13 conformance asserts compiled output matches the oracle within tolerance (correctness ground truth).

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Single-table view of all 14 phases (## 12..## 25). Durations sum to the Section 1 timeline (31.5 engineer-weeks, single-owner serial estimate). Key risks are the dominant failure mode per phase; exit criteria are the measurable gate that promotes the phase.

Phase Doc Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
0 ## 12 Workspace + 9 tc-* crate skeletons; Graph/PrimFunc/NDArray shells; PassManager/CodeGen/GraphExecutor stubs; CI + Makefile + Dockerfile 1.5 wk Crate dependency cycle (tc-runtime→tc-codegen only, no back-edges); cc toolchain absent in CI Smoke: 2-node add(a,b) Graph → hand-written PrimFunc → CBackend C kernel → compile via cc → run in GraphExecutor → bit-matches in-Rust reference add; cargo build + tcc --version green in CI/Docker
1 ## 13 Graph IR + op registry + TensorType/Shape/DType; textual-graph importer + ONNX-subset importer 2.5 wk ONNX opset drift (subset must be documented + version-pinned); ambiguous Dim::Dynamic naming collisions Round-trip: import → dump graph → re-parse is structurally identical on corpus of 12 models; op registry covers all GraphOp variants; importer rejects out-of-subset ops with a typed error
2 ## 14 PassManager/Pass infra; shape inference + type checking; verifier (every node typed) 2.0 wk Shape-inference non-termination on cyclic/under-constrained graphs; broadcast-rule corner cases Fixed-point shape/type inference converges on corpus; verifier asserts every GraphNode.ty == Some(_) post-pass; ≥40 inference unit fixtures pass incl. broadcast + reduce keepdims
3 ## 15 Const fold, CSE, DCE, algebraic simplification (canonicalization) 2.0 wk Const-fold numeric divergence from runtime kernels; CSE false-merge across differing TensorType Each pass idempotent (2nd run = no-op); folded constants match oracle within f32 rtol 1e-5; node-count reduction ≥30% on representative graph; DCE leaves only output-reachable nodes
4 ## 16 FusionPass, dominator-based grouping, fusion rules (elementwise + reduce/inject), FusionGroup → one PrimFunc 2.5 wk Incorrect dominator analysis fusing across a non-injective boundary; intermediate-buffer aliasing Fused output bit-equivalent to unfused (within tolerance) on op corpus; intermediate materializations eliminated for elementwise chains; ≥1 reduce successfully fused with its injective producers
5 ## 17 Layout/data-format transform (NCHW↔NHWC), contiguous-ization, cost-aware layout-convert insertion 2.0 wk Excess convert insertion thrashing perf; layout-tag inconsistency post-transform Minimal-cut convert insertion (no redundant back-to-back converts); transformed graph numerically equal to original; convert count provably ≤ baseline heuristic on Conv2d chains
6 ## 18 TIR: PrimFunc/Stmt/Expr/Buffer/IterVar; lower FusionGroup → TensorExpr + naive nested-loop PrimFunc 2.5 wk TensorExpr→loop-nest index miscompilation; Buffer shape/Scope mismatch Naive PrimFunc for each fused group evaluates to oracle within tolerance; IR builder emits well-formed Stmt tree (verifier passes); MatMul + Conv2d + Relu lowerable
7 ## 19 Schedule primitives I: split, reorder, fuse, tile, compute_at, inline 2.5 wk Schedule transform altering numerical result (must only change loop structure); invalid AxisId after split/fuse Each primitive preserves output bit-for-bit vs naive; split(ax,f) then fuse is identity; compute_at reduces a verified intermediate's footprint; axis-id bookkeeping consistent across transforms
8 ## 20 Schedule primitives II (perf): vectorize, unroll, parallel; Buffer Scope + codegen vectorization contract 2.0 wk Vectorize on non-divisible extent (tail handling); data race under parallel on shared Buffer Vectorized/parallel kernel matches scalar within tolerance; tail loop correct for non-multiple-of-lane extents; no data race (TSan clean) on parallel axis; vectorize honored only on innermost contiguous axis
9 ## 21 CPU codegen: scheduled TIR → C (CBackend) + portable-SIMD (SimdBackend); Kernel + KernelSig; compile via cc → Module 2.5 wk Generated C UB (aliasing, OOB index); ABI/KernelSig mismatch (ptr+shape+stride) Emitted C compiles -Wall -Werror clean; SimdBackend matches CBackend numerically; stable KernelSig (pointers+shapes+strides) verified by runtime loader; MatMul kernel ≥ scalar correctness
10 ## 22 Runtime: NDArray (strided/zero-copy), Storage+Allocator, liveness-based MemoryPlan, GraphExecutor topological dispatch 2.5 wk Use-after-free from buffer reuse; incorrect liveness over-aliasing live tensors MemoryPlan reuses storage only for non-overlapping liveness intervals (verified); peak memory ≤ naive by ≥25% on a multi-layer model; zero-copy reshape/slice shares Storage; executor output matches oracle end-to-end
11 ## 23 tc-vm: VmInstruction set, VmFunc, Interpreter, control flow (If/Goto/Call), dynamic shapes, Device, async dispatch 2.5 wk Register lifetime bugs / leaks in interpreter loop; dynamic-shape alloc divergence from static path VM executes a dynamic-control-flow model matching oracle; Dim::Dynamic resolved at runtime correctly; same Kernels dispatched as GraphExecutor; no register/Storage leak over 10⁴-iteration loop
12 ## 24 Auto-tuning: schedule search space, cost model (analytical + measured), per-fused-kernel tuner 2.0 wk Search-space explosion / unbounded tune time; cost model mis-ranking schedules Tuner improves a kernel ≥1.5× over default schedule within a bounded trial budget (e.g. ≤500 measured trials); cost model rank-correlation ≥0.7 vs measured; all candidate schedules numerically valid
13 ## 25 Conformance vs NumPy/reference oracle, differential testing, fuzzing (graph + schedule generators), GFLOP/s benchmarks vs naive baseline 2.5 wk Tolerance creep masking real bugs; fuzzer-found crashes/miscompiles Full op + model corpus within f32 rtol 1e-5 / atol 1e-6; 10⁶ fuzz iterations (graph+schedule) no crash + no numerical violation; MatMul ≥40% of single-core BLAS GFLOP/s; benchmark report committed

Total: 31.5 engineer-weeks, matching the Section 1 timeline.

Phase-0 done vs full done. Phase-0 done (## 12) means the end-to-end skeleton compiles and the add(a,b) smoke path traverses every layer — Graph → hand-written PrimFunc → CBackend C kernel → cc compile → GraphExecutor execution → numerical match against the in-Rust reference — proving the nine-crate spine and the build/CI/Docker plumbing are sound, with all real compiler logic still stubbed. Full done (through ## 25) means the complete pipeline — ONNX-subset/textual import, the full pass + fusion + layout suite, TIR lowering, the complete schedule primitive set, CBackend+SimdBackend codegen, the GraphExecutor + tc-vm Interpreter runtimes with liveness-based MemoryPlan, and the auto-tuner — passes the conformance corpus within f32 rtol 1e-5 / atol 1e-6, survives 10⁶ differential-fuzz iterations with zero crashes and zero numerical violations, and hits ≥40% of single-core BLAS GFLOP/s on MatMul; only the explicitly PhaseDeferred items (GPU/CUDA codegen, full ONNX op set, distributed execution) remain out of scope.
